->and of an Alchemist. 77<-

[I] am half dead, for he has tortured me until death. **Alchemist.** Oh, you got exactly what you deserved, for you are disobedient. **Mercury.** <note>Mercury represents both the metallic element quicksilver and a witty, trickster spirit in alchemical lore.</note> To no true Philosopher have I ever been disobedient; rather, by the very property of my nature, I laugh at and mock the unwise fools. **Alchemist.** What do you think of me? **Mercury.** Oh Lord, you are a glorious, excellent man, a greatly enlightened Philosopher; in your appearance, you surpass even Hermes <note>A reference to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>. <margin>Thus I will, <note>original: "Sic volo,"</note></margin> **Alchemist.** Yes, well said; I am a learned man, but yet I will not <margin>thus I command. <note>original: "sic jubeo." This is a famous Latin tag used to denote someone acting out of sheer will rather than reason.</note></margin> boast about myself. My own wife often says to me that I am quite a skillful Philosopher—so much has this woman seen in me. **Mercury.** I well believe that, for thus must such "Philosophers" be: so full of vanity and wit that they turn into fools. **Alchemist.** Well then, tell me: what shall I do with you? How must I prepare the **Philosophical Stone** <term>Philosophical Stone: the legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting immortality</term> out of you? **Mercury.** Oh, my Lord Philosopher, I do not know that. You are the Philosopher, and I am but a servant to the Philosophers. Whatever they wish to make out of me, that is their choice; I offer them obedience as much as I can. **Alchemist.** You must tell me how I should deal with you, and whether I can prepare the Philosophical Stone from you. **Mercury.** If you know how, you will succeed; if you do not know, you will fail...

The exchange highlights the contrast between the true "Philosophers" of nature and the "fools" who merely seek gold through vanity.</summary>

78 ->Dialogue of Nature and Mercury<-

you are lacking; you will learn nothing from me if you do not already know it, my Lord Philosopher.

**Alchemist.** What? Do you speak to me thus, as if to a lowly person? Do you not know that I have worked for great princes and lords, and have been considered a clever <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: "Philosophus"; in this context, it refers to an alchemist or natural scientist</note> among them?

**Mercury.** I well believe you, my Lord, and for that reason I speak rightly; for I still stink from the filth with which you have befouled me in your "beautiful" works.

**Alchemist.** Then tell me, are you the <term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term>? <note>Alchemists distinguished between common quicksilver and a sophisticated "philosophical" mercury which was believed to be the true "First Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

**Mercury.** I am Mercury; but whether I am the "Mercury of the Philosophers," that you must know for yourself.

**Alchemist.** Just tell me if you are the true Mercury, or if there is another?

**Mercury.** I am quicksilver, or Mercury, yet there is still another.

With that, Mercury vanished. The Alchemist cried out after him and called, but no one would give him an answer. In that moment, he thought to himself and said: "Truly, I am a righteous man; Mercury has spoken with me himself; surely he loves me." 

With that, he began to labor again with the utmost diligence: he <term>sublimed</term> <note>heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again to purify it</note> the quicksilver, distilled it, <term>calcined</term> <note>reducing a metal to powder through intense heat</note> it, <term>precipitated</term> <note>causing a solid to separate from a liquid solution</note> it, and dissolved it with many strange methods and manners, also with various "waters" <note>refers to acids or chemical solvents</note>. Yet it was all in vain, just as before; he wasted his time along with the expense; therefore, he finally cursed

->and of an Alchemist. 79<-

...finally [blaming] Mercury and Nature, saying that they had conceived and given birth to his failure. But when Nature heard this, she called Mercury and spoke to him: "What harm have you done to this man? Why does he curse me on your account, and speak so ill of me? Why do you not yield what you owe?" At this, Mercury excused himself most highly. Nevertheless, Nature commanded him that he should be obedient to the **Sons of Wisdom** <note>Technical term: "Sons of Wisdom" refers to true alchemical adepts who understand the spiritual and natural laws, as opposed to "puffers" or clumsy laborers.</note> who sought him. Mercury promised to do so, and said to his mother Nature: "Dear mother, what is one to do with fools? Or who can satisfy their desires?" At this, Nature smirked <note>original: "schmächlächelt" — a scornful or subtle, mocking smile.</note> and departed from there.

Mercury, however, became angry and resentful toward the Alchemist; he also retired to his accustomed place. After several days had passed, it occurred to the Alchemist again that he had forgotten something in his labors; he turned once more to the **quicksilver** <term>Mercury in its liquid metallic form</term> and intended to mix it with pig dung. But Mercury was enraged, because the Alchemist had already falsely accused him before his mother Nature, and he said to him: "You fool, what do you want to do with me? Why have you accused me?" 

**Alchemist:** "See, are you present, you whom I seek?" 

**Mercury:** "Yes, it is indeed I: but no blind man can see me." 

**Alchemist:** "I am not blind."

yourself [catchword]

# Page 80

## ... of Mercury
<note>original: "Mercurij." In alchemy, Mercury (or Quicksilver) represents the fluid, volatile principle of matter and is one of the "Three Primes" alongside Sulfur and Salt.</note>

<margin>If he himself is ignorant of this, he is far removed from this art. — Geber
<note>original: "ipso ignoraverit, hic multum remotus est ab hac arte." Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century scholar often called the "Father of Alchemy." This note emphasizes that understanding Mercury is essential to the craft.</note></margin>

The **Alchemist** <note>original: "Alchymist"</note> must understand the nature of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"</note> to succeed. As the **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophus." In this context, "The Philosopher" often refers to Aristotle or a generalized wise authority in the Hermetic tradition.</note> writes, this substance is the beginning and the end of the work. Among the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis"</note>, there is much debate, yet all **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophos"</note> agree that without a pure **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophum"</note>, the stone cannot be made. 

<margin>Behold the man!
<note>original: "Ecce homo!" While traditionally a biblical reference to Christ, in alchemical manuscripts this phrase often signals the appearance of the "reborn" or perfected matter at a specific stage of the chemical process.</note></margin>

O **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophe"</note>, look closely at the **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note> and how it transforms within the vessel.

...tortured in the worst way, in a devilish manner.
**Alchemist.** Oh, what do I hear! Truly you are the
Devil himself! For I have performed everything according to

work with expert skill. **Mercury.** With "expert
skill" you can do it—you simply do too much to it, and more
than you know or read. For the **Philosophers**
say: One should mix **nature with nature**,
and outside of nature they wish to have
nothing foreign. But you have already
mixed me with the vilest things, and with
dung. **Alchemist.** I perform
nothing outside of nature, but I sow the
seed into its earth, just as the **Philosophers** command.
**Mercury.** You sow me in dung; and when the <margin>What a man sows, that shall he reap. <note>original Latin: "Quod quis serit, illud metit."</note></margin>
time of the harvest arrives, I fly away <note>A reference to the volatility of quicksilver (mercury), which evaporates or "flies" when heated, often leaving the alchemist with nothing but residue.</note>, and
you find nothing but filth to cut or
to harvest. **Alchemist.** Yet the **Philosophers** have
written that their **Matter** <term>Matter: The "Materia Prima" or base substance required to begin the Great Work of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term> is to be found in manure.
**Mercury.** What the **Philosophers** wrote <margin>The letter <note>original Latin: "Litera." Likely referring to the "letter of the law" or the literal, surface-level meaning of a text as opposed to its spiritual or metaphorical truth.</note></margin>

32
->**Dialogue of Nature and Mercury**<-

**Mercury.** You say that I am inconstant, but I will refute that for you: I am constant to a steadfast artist, and I am <term>fixed|Stable and resistant to the heat of fire, rather than evaporating.</term> to a fixed master. But you and those like you are changeable and inconstant; you go from one thing to another, and from one material into another.

**Alchemist.** Therefore, tell me: are you that same Mercury of whom the Philosophers have written, saying that—together with Sulfur and Salt—he is the beginning of all things, or must one seek another? <note>Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt are the "Three Primes" (Tria Prima), the basic building blocks of all matter in Paracelsian alchemy.</note>

**Mercury.** It is true that "the fruit does not fall far from the tree," yet I by no means desire to seek my own glory. I am exactly the one I was before. But my age and my years are different.

**Alchemist.** Now you please me, because you say that you are somewhat old; for I have always striven for such a one who might be more mature and fixed, so that I might more easily reach the end <note>The "end" refers to the successful completion of the Great Work.</note> with him.

**Mercury.** You seek me in vain and to no purpose in my old age, you who did not know me in my youth.

**Alchemist.** What? Should I not have known you, since I have worked with you in many ways at all times—as you yourself have witnessed—and I will not yet cease until I have obtained the <term>Philosophers' Stone|A legendary substance believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life.</term>?

**Mercury.** Oh, poor me! What should I begin to do? I fear that perhaps I must again be stained and defiled with coal and dung. <note>Mercury often complains in these texts about the "dirty" and ill-conceived experiments of amateur alchemists, whom the masters called "puffers."</note> Thus has
                                                                            
<summary>The personified Mercury defends his stability against the Alchemist's accusations of inconstancy, explaining that he only becomes "fixed" for a true master. Mercury laments that the Alchemist seeks his mature state without understanding his "youth" or true nature.</summary>

I have a new cross <note>The speaker here is Mercury, personified as a suffering spirit. In this context, a "cross" refers to a heavy burden or a state of torment.</note>; woe is me, poor thing. O
Master Philosopher, I beg you, that you would
at the very least not mix me with sow-dung <note>original: "Sewkoht." In early alchemy, animal waste was used in "digestion" processes to provide a low, steady heat through fermentation.</note>,
otherwise I am at my wit’s end <note>original: "hab ich das letze auff der Mühlen," literally "I have the last at the mill," a German idiom for being at the point of total ruin or death.</note>,

that I should do? Am I not sufficiently plagued by you?

yet you always return to your old essence.

you would have me: if you want me physically as
a body, I become a body; if you want
me as a powder, I am and become

84 ->Dialogue of Nature / of Mercury<-

how I should or could humble myself more and sufficiently, than when I turn to powder and ashes.
**Alchemist.** Therefore tell me, who are you in your **inner root**? 
**Mercury.** Now I am compelled, and must speak with you from the depths; and if you wish, you can well understand me. You see my form, of which there is no need to tell you anything. But since you ask me about my inner core and center-point: the heart of my inner **Center** <note>The "Center" in alchemy often refers to the essential, unchanging spark or "seed" of a substance.</note> is the most fixed, immortal, and penetrating. In it is the power and rest of my Lord. I myself, however, am the Way and the **Messenger**: <note>Mercury, or Hermes, was the messenger of the gods; in alchemy, he represents the medium through which transformation occurs.</note> the stranger and the native. I am the most faithful to all my friends; I never abandon those who follow me; with them I remain, with them I die; an immortal body and thing am I. I die indeed when I am killed: but at the judgment of a wise judge, I rise again.
**Alchemist.** Are you the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term>? <note>The legendary substance sought by alchemists to transmute base metals into gold and achieve spiritual perfection.</note>
**Mercury.** My mother is; from her grows such a unique, artful thing.

<margin>
My brother has in his will or in his fleece that which you desire. <note>original Latin: "Frater meus in velle seu vellere suo habet, quod cupis." This likely refers to the "Golden Fleece" of myth, used as a metaphor for the alchemical goal.</note>
</margin>

But my brother, who lives in the castle, has in his will what the philosopher's desire is.
**Alchemist.** But are you old?
**Mercury.** My mother bore me, and yet I am older than my mother. <note>This paradox suggests that the spiritual essence of Mercury exists eternally, even before the physical matter ("mother") that contains it.</note>
**Alchemist.** What devil would want to understand you, since you do not answer my purpose, but bring forth nothing but riddles. Tell me, whether you are the **Source**, from which

->and of an Alchemist. 85<-

of which **Bernard, the Count of Trevisan** <note>original: "Bernhardus der Grave von Tervis." Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490) was a famous Italian alchemist known for his allegorical "Parable of the Fountain."</note> has written. **Mercury.** I am not the **fountain** <note>original: "Fontina." This refers to the "Alchemical Fountain," a symbol for the solvent used to dissolve metals.</note>, but a water; the fountain has surrounded me. **Alchemist.** Is the gold dissolved in you, since you are a water? **Mercury.** That which is with me, I love as my friend; and to that which is born with me, I give nourishment; what is naked and bare, I cover with my **wings** <note>In alchemy, wings represent "volatility"—the ability of a substance to evaporate or turn into gas when heated. Mercury is the "winged" element.</note>. **Alchemist.** I see that you cannot be reasoned with. I ask about one thing, and you give me answers about other and strange things. If you do not answer better, I will truly set to work with you again. **Mercury.** Oh Lord, I pray you, be merciful; now I will gladly tell what I know. **Alchemist.** Then tell me if you fear the **fire**. **Mercury.** I am myself a fire. **Alchemist.** Why then do you flee the fire? **Mercury.** My spirit falls in love with the **fire-spirit** <note>likely referring to Sulfur, the "active" masculine principle that "fixes" the "volatile" Mercury.</note>, and as much as possible, one follows after the other. **Alchemist.** And where do you go then, when you rise up with the fire? **Mercury.** Know that every stranger always desires his **fatherland**; and when he arrives back there, from whence he first departed, then he rests; and he always comes home wiser than when he left. **Alchemist.** Do you then ever return to us? **Mercury.** I return, but in another form.

<meta>This page continues the "Dialogue of the Nature of Mercury," a classic alchemical text where the element Mercury (quicksilver) is personified and speaks to a seeker (the Alchemist) about its spiritual and physical properties.</meta>

16      **Dialogue on the Nature of Mercury**

**Alchemist.** I do not understand what you say, much less the fire of which you speak.
**Mercury.** Whoever knows the fire of my heart sees that fire (namely, the proper warmth <note>original: *gebührende wärmb*; this refers to the carefully regulated heat of an alchemical furnace</note>) is my food and nourishment; and the more the spirit of my heart is fed with fire, the more fruitful and rich <note>literally "fat" or "fertile"</note> it becomes. Its death and passing away is thereafter the life of all things found in this, my kingdom.
**Alchemist.** Are you great or powerful?
**Mercury.** Consider me as an example: from a thousand droplets I become one thing; from a single one, I divide myself into many thousands of droplets. And even as you have me physically before your eyes and know how to play with me <note>a reference to the fluid, "living" movement of liquid quicksilver, which was often called "living water"</note>, you can divide me into as many pieces as you please, yet I will become one thing again. What then should my spirit (the internal heart) accomplish? It produces uncountably many thousands from the very smallest part at all times.
**Alchemist.** How should I behave toward you, then, so that I may prepare you in this manner?
**Mercury.** Internally I am a fire; fire is my food. But the life of fire is the air; without air, the fire is extinguished. The fire surpasses the air. Therefore, I have no rest nor peace, and no common air can bind or retain me. Join air to air <note>In alchemy, "air" often refers to volatile substances; joining them suggests a process of stabilization</note>, so that they both become one and are weighty or heavy.
**Alchemist.** What will finally come of it?
**Mercury.** The overflow—

<margin>The sum of the entire work <note>original Latin: *Summa totius ope-* (likely *operis*)</note></margin>

fix <note>original: *fige*, a catchword referring to the alchemical process of "fixation," making a volatile substance permanent and solid</note>

<summary>This page features a philosophical dialogue where Mercury explains its nature as a transformative substance that thrives on fire and can be infinitely divided yet remain unified. It introduces the concept of "fixing" volatile elements (air) to make them "heavy" or stable.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, alchemy, philosophical fire, fixation, quicksilver, alchemical elements, transformation</keywords>

**and of an Alchemist. 87**

is separated; what remains behind, <margin>reported from the tripod <note>original: "ex tripode relata." **88 Dialogue of Nature and Mercury**

...alchemists <note>The word fragment "miſten" is the conclusion of "Alchymisten" from the previous page.</note>, of whose number you are also one, show me—against all my will and knowledge—every kind of adversity. ->and an Alchemist. 89<-

...and useful it is; with it he increases my goods and heals the bodies of my children. **Alchemist:** I do the same for him. **Nature:** You show me nothing but resistance, and you work against my will with my children; where you should bring me to life, you kill me; where you should make me **fixed** <term>fixed|In alchemy, 'fixing' refers to making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand heat without evaporating or changing</term>, you elevate and **sublime** <term>sublimation|The process of heating a substance until it becomes vapor and then collecting the purified solid that forms when it cools</term> me; where you should **calcine** <term>calcination|The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a fine powder or ash</term>, you **distill**. You behave this way especially toward my most humble and obedient son, **Mercury** <note>original Latin-inflected form: "Mercurio"</note>, whom you torture with so many sharp and caustic waters <note>likely referring to strong acids like nitric acid or sulfuric acid, which alchemists called 'strong waters'</note> and so many poisonous things. **Alchemist:** Ah, then from now on I will place him most kindly and gently only into **digestion** <term>digestion|A method of applying gentle, steady heat over a long period, mimicking the natural warmth of a stomach or a nesting bird</term>.

**Nature:** Quite right—if only you actually know how! If not, you do not harm him, but rather yourself and your own purse. For it is all the same to him; he mixes just as well with dung as with gold. A precious stone is always magnificent and good; it is not defiled by filth, even if it has been mixed with it. For once it is washed away, it is the same precious stone as before. **Alchemist:** But I would dearly like to have the **Philosophers' Stone** and know how to make it. **Nature:** If that is your intent, you must not boil and roast my son in such a way. You should know that I have many sons and daughters; I am also well-disposed toward those who seek me, if they are worthy of me. **Alchemist:** Then tell me: what kind of Mercury is that?

## **Dialogue of Nature and Mercury**

**Nature.** Know that I have only one such unique son—only one, I say, out of seven—who is the very first, and who is also all in all. He is everything, I say, who was yet a single one and is yet nothing; after this, his number is complete and whole. Within him are the four elements <note>The classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth</note>, and yet he himself is no element. He is a spirit and yet has no body. He is a man and yet takes the place of a woman—or rather, he is of both masculine and feminine nature; that is, a hermaphrodite <term>hermaphrodite: in alchemy, a 'rebis' or dual-natured substance representing the union of opposites</term> or a hybrid <note>original: 'Zwiſpelkind', an archaic term for a creature of dual nature</note>. He is a boy and yet carries manly weapons. He is an animal and yet has wings like a bird. He is a poison and yet heals leprosy. He is life itself and yet kills everything. He is a king, yet another possesses his kingdom. He flies away along with the fire. He is a water and yet does not wet. He is an earth and yet is sown. He is an air and yet lives in the water.

**Alch.** Now I see that I know nothing, but I dare not say so; for I would lose my reputation and praise, and none of my friends would think anything of me anymore. Nevertheless, I will speak and act as if I knew a great deal, otherwise no one would give me so much as a piece of bread, for there are many who hope for great wealth from me.

**Nature.** But what if you carry on this way for a long time? What kind of end will it come to? In the end, every one of your friends will want back what is theirs.

**Alchemist.** I will feed them all with good hope—as long as [Catchword: as long as]

<summary>Nature describes the paradoxical and dualistic qualities of the alchemical Mercury, while the Alchemist confesses his ignorance and his reliance on deception to maintain his reputation and funding.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, hermaphrodite, four elements, alchemical paradoxes, fraud, reputation, Zwiſpelkind, philosophical mercury</keywords>

102 On Sulfur/ the second Principle
**On Sulfur.**
**The Second Principle of Mineral things-**

Sulfur is not the least among the principles and origins of minerals, because it is a part of the metal; indeed, it is the most excellent part of the Stone of the Sages <note>original: "Stains der Waiſen," more commonly known today as the Philosopher’s Stone</note>, about which many ancient Philosophers have left behind beautiful, diverse, and truthful accounts in their writings. Even Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century polymath and alchemist whose Latinized name was Geber</note> himself, in his book *The Sum of Perfection* <note>original: "Summariſchen vollkommenheit," referring to the *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii*, a foundational alchemical text</note>, in the 28th Chapter, says: By God most high, <margin>It is light from light <note>original: "Eſt lumen à lumine"</note></margin> it illuminates every body, for it is a light from the light, and a <term>tincture|in alchemy, a potent substance or "elixir" capable of transforming and perfecting base metals</term>. But before we begin to discuss it, <margin>The origin of the principles <note>original: "Origo principiorum"</note></margin> it seems advisable to us to first describe the source of these principles, especially...

Of metals, according to the opinion of the old philosophers <note>The "old philosophers" typically refers to Aristotle and the early Alchemists who believed all metals were composed of two principles: Sulphur and Mercury.</note>, there are two: Sulphur and Mercury. According to the younger ones, however, there are three: Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury <note>The "younger" school refers to Paracelsians, who added Salt as the third principle, forming the *Tria Prima*.</note>. These beginnings, however, arise from the four elements, from whose roots we must specifically begin. Thus, the students of this holy science <note>Alchemy was often called the "Holy Science" or *Ars Sacra* because it was seen as a way to understand the divine mechanics of creation.</note> should note that there are four elements: and that each of these four holds another within its center and midpoint, from which it receives its elemental nature. These are the four pillars of the world, which in the creation of the world were separated from the mixed Chaos by Divine Wisdom. These also hold this world-building in balance and proportion through their opposing effects, and through the influence of heavenly powers, they bring forth all things beneath and above the Earth, of which we will speak in its proper place. We will turn to our subject and first treat the nearest element, the Earth.

**Of the Element of Earth.**

The Earth is an element very worthy in its quality and magnificence. In this element rest the remaining three, but especially Fire. It is the foremost element for hiding and for revealing what is entrusted to it: it is dense and porous, heavy because of its smallness <note>In the geocentric model of the universe, the Earth was considered a single point or "center" compared to the vastness of the heavens.</note>, and light by its nature. It is the <margin>Earth is the center <note>original: "Terra est centrum"</note></margin> center of the world, and also of the other elements.

->112<-

<margin>There are two poles of the world, the arctic and the antarctic.</margin>

Regarding the nature of the great currents and how the waters are directed across the globe, one must consider the power of the **Pole**. It is established that the **Poles**—the two points upon which the world turns—are essential to this motion. These are the **Arctic** in the north and the **Antarctic** in the south. Just as the lodestone draws iron, these regions exert a profound influence on the flow of the sea.

<margin>Through the pores of the earth.</margin>

Furthermore, we must understand that this water does not merely travel across the surface of the sea. It also moves <margin>through the pores of the earth</margin> <note>Early modern natural philosophers believed the Earth was a porous body, containing a network of subterranean channels and "pores" through which water filtered and circulated, similar to the veins in a human body.</note>. Each **Pole** acts as a primary anchor for this circulation. Because the **Poles** are the cold foundations of our world, they govern the density and direction of the tides. 

When the water originates from the **Arctic Pole** <note>original: "Polo Arctico"</note>, it carries with it the cold temperament of the north, eventually being drawn back toward the opposite **Pole** to complete the great cycle of the world’s waters.

<summary>This page describes the influence of the Arctic and Antarctic poles on global water currents, proposing that water circulates both on the surface and through subterranean "pores."</summary>

### Of Mineral Things. 113

...only impress a power and spiritual influence, which has no weight or heaviness.

Therefore, the waters are not generated <note>The author is arguing against the idea that water is constantly being "created" or "born" anew; instead, he proposes a system of circulation.</note>, but are spread from the middle of the sea through the secret passages of the earth into the whole world. From these natural foundations, the philosophers have invented many kinds of instruments, water conduits, and fountain-works; for it is known that, by natural law, water does not rise higher than its source. If it were not so by nature, art <term>art: translated from the German "Kunst," referring to human technology, engineering, or alchemical skill</term> would never be able to achieve it, for art follows nature; and what is not already in nature cannot be attained through art. Thus water, as we have said before, is not driven higher than the place from which it is taken.

Take for example a wine-siphon or a "wine-stealer" <note>original: "Weinſtäler," a glass tube used to draw samples of wine from the top of a barrel</note>, with which one draws wine out of a cask. You should therefore finally know that no water springs or rivers are generated from the stars, but from the middle of the sea, where they all eventually return. They thus maintain a steady, everlasting cycle; for if this were not so, nothing at all would be produced either in or on the earth—indeed, the destruction of the world would follow.

But so that no one may object that all sea water is salty while all spring water is sweet, one should know the reason: the springs are distilled through the passages of the earth, and percolate <note>The German "durchringen" suggests a forceful straining or wringing through a medium.</note> for many miles through narrow places and much sand...

<summary>This page explains an early modern theory of the hydrological cycle, arguing that all fresh water originates from the sea and circulates through underground passages, being purified of salt by the earth's natural filtration.</summary>
<keywords>hydrology, water cycle, filtration, nature vs art, wine-stealer, geocentrism, distillation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Three Principles" of alchemy—Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—focusing here on how the movement of water through the earth's interior creates mineral springs and thermal baths.</meta>

114

## Of Sulfur / The Second Principle

...[pass] through, and thus, losing their salty nature, they become sweet—an example from which the invention of cisterns was derived. However, in some places, the veins and fissures in the earth are larger and wider, allowing salt water to penetrate through. This is the origin of brine springs and salt water sources, such as those in Hall in Swabia, and Saxony, and many other parts of Germany. In certain locations, the water is boiled away by internal heat, so that the salt remains behind in the sand and soil, while the water drains away through other channels, as seen in Poland at Wiślica <note>original: "Viſlitza"</note> and Bochnia.

Likewise, when waters flow through hot, sulfurous, and constantly burning areas, <margin>The origin of thermal baths <note>original Latin: "Origo thermarū."</note></margin> they become heated, and warm baths arise from them. For deep within the earth, there are places where Nature melts, distills, and separates sulfurous ores when they are ignited by the central fire <note>The "central fire" refers to the early modern belief in a subterranean heat source at the earth's core, which was thought to fuel volcanoes and geysers.</note>. When water flows through such burning regions, it is heated by them—more or less depending on how closely it passes—and then it emerges at the surface <term>to day</term> <note>In historical mining and geology, "to day" or "to the day" refers to reaching the surface or open air.</note>, retaining its sulfurous taste, just as a cooked broth tastes of the meat.

The same happens when water flows through mineral-rich deposits, such as copper or alum <note>Alum is a naturally occurring mineral salt historically used in dyeing, tanning, and medicine.</note>; it acquires a flavor from them. The Creator of all things is just such a distiller, in whose hand are this flask and <term>alembic</term> <note>An alembic is a classic piece of alchemical equipment used for distilling liquids, consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</note>. It is by following His example that all methods of distillation were discovered by the philosophers <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to natural philosophers or alchemists who studied the workings of the physical world.</note>—an art which the Almighty and Merciful God Himself...

122      **Of Sulfur / the Second Principle**

<margin><term>Flint</term> <note>Original: *Silex*. The author uses flint as a metaphor for how "Sulfur" (the active, fiery principle) remains hidden within matter until it is provoked or "ignited."</note></margin> is not felt nor seen, unless it be awakened and ignited through movement, so that it appears; likewise, the Fire in which the Holy Majesty of our Creator exists is immovable, unless it is stirred by the very will of the Most High and directed wherever His holy will intends. For by the will of the highest Creator, the most powerful and awe-inspiring <note>The original German *schröcklichste* can mean "terrible," but in this 17th-century context, it carries the sense of "awe-inspiring" or "formidable" power.</note> impulse occurs. 

Consider an analogy: when a potentate of this world sits in his pomp and glory, dear [reader], what peace and stillness surrounds him! And even if one of his servants stirs, it is but the single movement of one or another, which no one notices. But when the Lord himself moves, there is a general commotion <note>Original: *rumor*, here meaning a great noise or public stir rather than a spoken rumor.</note> and uprising, and the entire court moves with him. 

Thus, when the Supreme Monarch, the King of all Kings, the Creator of all things (after whose example the princes of this world are modeled) exercises His authority—God help us!—what a movement that is, what a trembling and quaking, when the entire assembly of the Heavenly Hosts moves around Him. 

But someone might ask how we know these things, since human understanding cannot fathom heavenly matters. To them we answer that these things are known to all philosophers; indeed, incomprehensible Wisdom has breathed it into them, so that everything [is made] according to the 
                                                                                                pattern <note>Original: *ebenbild*. This refers to the theory of the "Microcosm," where the earthly world is a smaller reflection of the divine "Macrocosm."</note>

Until now, this has been a matter of debate. But many hundreds of years ago, God inspired the pious philosophers to understand that this is actually natural, which you shall understand from us in this way: Paradise was, and is, a place created by the Almighty Creator of all things out of the true, individual elements—yet these were the very purest, <term>tempered<gloss>Perfectly balanced so that no single quality (like heat or cold) overwhelmed the others, preventing decay.</gloss></term>, and glorified in the highest perfection. Thus, everything that was in Paradise and created from those same elements is indestructible. Man was also created there out of those same indestructible and perfectly proportioned elements, so that he could in no way be destroyed. Therefore, he became a partaker of immortality, in that God

Because he could not live without nourishment, it was necessary for him to seek his sustenance from the corrupted elements. Through this, those pure elements of his [original] creation were infected and gradually brought to ruin and decay, until one quality overcame the other, and the corruption of the entire frame, weakness, and finally separation <note>A common euphemism for death, referring to the soul separating from the physical body.</note> and death followed. 

Subsequently, those born outside of Paradise in these perishable elements from corrupted seed have come much closer to this decay and death, because the seed which grows from perishable nourishment cannot be long-lasting. The further humanity [moves in time] from the expulsion from Paradise, the closer they come to destructibility; hence the shortening of human life. It will reach the point where—<margin>Good words, I pray <note>original: "Bona verba quæso." Among us, however, they hasten—

Therefore, you have the way in which the destructive elements in every thing fall and are separated when one overcomes another; for then, through the first separation, putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the alchemical stage of "nigredo" or blackening, where matter is broken down by decay to be reborn</term> occurs. ->of all things<-                                     137

...a distance <note>In alchemical theory, this refers to the 'proximate' and 'remote' matter of substances.</note>: the near one is **Sulfur** and **Mercury**; the distant ones are the **four Elements**, from which God alone can create anything. Therefore, leave the elements alone, for you cannot and are not able to make anything from them except these **three beginnings** <note>Known as the *Tria Prima*—Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt—the fundamental components of all matter according to Paracelsus.</note>: Considering that Nature itself brings nothing else forth from them. Since you can bring nothing other than these **three beginnings** out of the elements, what use is this futile labor to seek or make that which has already been created by Nature? Is it not better to walk three miles than four? Be satisfied, therefore, that you have these **three beginnings**, from which Nature makes all things on Earth and under the Earth, and which you can also find perfectly in all things. From this proper separation and composition, Nature gives birth to stones as well as metals in the mineral kingdom; in the vegetable kingdom, trees, herbs, and everything else; and in animals, also **Body**, **Spirit**, and **Soul**, which is principally compared to the **Philosophical Work** <note>The *Magnum Opus*, or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>: the **Body** is the Earth; the **Spirit** is the Water; and the **Soul** is the Fire, or the **Sulfur of gold** <note>In this context, 'Sulfur' refers to the active, internal 'fire' or soul of the metal, not the common yellow mineral.</note>. The size of the body is increased by the spirit, but the power is increased by the fire. However, because there is more of the spirit by weight than of the fire, the spirit is raised up and suppresses the fire, drawing it to itself; and thus each of them grows in power, while the earth, which is between the two, increases in weight.

**Fires**, it is enough that it has nourishment; and the external fire is its food, and is to be reckoned just as wood is against elementary fire; and according to such nourishment, it grows and increases itself. Common Mercury dissolves neither Gold nor Silver in such a way that it cannot be separated from them again: but our Quicksilver dissolves Gold and Silver, and will never again be separated from them for all eternity, just as when water is poured into water. white, reaching a **crystalline clarity**. **Common Mercury** is precipitated <note>original: *præcipitirt*; meaning to be chemically separated or settled out from a solution</note> into a yellow powder and an "evil" <note>In alchemical terms, "evil" or "bad" usually refers to substances containing impurities, such as common sulfur, which were thought to "corrupt" metals</note> sulfur; however, **our Mercury** <note>The "Mercury of the Sages," a refined, theoretical substance central to alchemy, as opposed to the liquid metal found in nature</note> is, by means of heat, converted into a snow-white, good, **fixed** <term>fixed: a substance that is stable in fire and does not evaporate or fly away when heated</term>, and liquid sulfur. Such a person seeks the art by chance; and if he occasionally and often by chance gets the right Matter of our Quicksilver <note>original: "Quecksilbers"</note> into his hands, he stops where he should begin, and thus loses by chance what he found by chance, for he does not know upon what he should ground his intent. Indeed, this science is a gift of the Most High; and unless God reveals it to someone through high understanding or a good friend, it is recognized with great difficulty. The judgments of the Lord are a deep abyss, yet in our misfortune, we have had to marvel greatly at Divine providence. The alchemist took heart and answered: "Sir, just as a hungry person always thinks of bread, so do I constantly think of the Stone of the Sages."

**Alchemist.** Sir, is he the same everywhere?
**Voice.** <note>The original abbreviation "Stiin." likely refers to "Stimme" (Voice), a common character in alchemical dialogues representing a guiding spirit or personified Nature.</note> As far as his nature is concerned, he is the same, but in prison <note>The "prison" refers to the crude, unrefined state of the material (Materia Prima) as it is found in the earth or before chemical purification.</note> he changes himself; yet his heart is always pure, though his clothes are very stained. **Alchemist.** Sir, was he also ever free? <note>original: "ledig," which carries the double meaning of being unmarried and being unbound or released.</note> **Voice.** Yes, certainly; especially in those times when the wise men lived, who had great friendship with his mother. **Alchemist.** Who were they then? **Voice.** Countless many; it was Hermes, <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of Hermetic philosophy.</note> who was entirely at one with his mother: after him many kings and princes, and no less many other wise men, such as in our times Aristotle, Avicenna, Paracelsus, etc., who have liberated him. And so, full of shame, so that they might not be held as unworthy (as they indeed are, due to their coarse understanding), they shout and rail against this art. 

This science hates such fellows and always shows them the beginning at the end. But we gladly admit to the unworthy that there is nothing to this art; however, to the lovers of virtue, true seekers, and children of wisdom, we praise—

# Concluding Discourse.

<margin>Every end is bounded by its own beginning. <note>original: "Omnis finis supra suū principium terminatur."</note></margin>

**E**very seeker of **this art** <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, the practice of transforming matter.</note> should, above all things, examine with mature reflection the creation, operation, and powers of the four elements within his process. For if he does not know this origin and nature, he will not come to a knowledge of the **principles** <note>In this context, the "beginnings" or fundamental components of matter.</note>, nor will he ever recognize the true matter of the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to transmute base metals into gold.</note>; much less will he achieve a good result, because every end arises from its beginning. He who well knows what he begins, also well knows what he will end. For the origin of the elements is a **Chaos** <note>The primal, unformed mass of matter from which God created the universe.</note>, out of which God, the Creator of all things, created and separated the elements—which is the work of God alone. From these elements, however, Nature brings forth the principles of things; and that belongs, according to the will of God, to Nature alone. Afterward, from these principles, Nature makes minerals and ores and all things, from which the artist—in imitation of Nature—is capable of many wonderful things. This is because Nature brings forth ores, metals, and all manner of things from these principles, which are: <term>Salt</term> <note>In alchemy, Salt represents the principle of body, solidity, and fixity.</note>, <term>Sulfur</term> <note>Sulfur represents the principle of soul, oiliness, and inflammability.</note>, and <term>Mercury</term> <note>Mercury represents the principle of spirit, fluidity, and volatility.</note>. And Nature does not simply make a metal out of the elements, but through the principles, which are an intermediary between the elements and the metals.

<margin>The principles are the intermediary between the elements and the metals. <note>original: "Principia sunt medium inter elementa & metalla."</note></margin>

Therefore, if Nature does not do it...

<summary>This page explains the alchemical hierarchy of creation, asserting that God created the elements from Chaos, and Nature then uses those elements to create the Three Principles (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) before finally forming metals. It emphasizes that the alchemist must follow this natural order to succeed.</summary>

->### Concluding Discourse<-

...does, much less will **the art** <term>the art: a standard term for alchemy, referring to the human imitation or perfection of natural processes</term> be able to do so: and attention must be paid not only in this example, but in all **natural processes**, to the intermediate disposition: for this reason, we have described in this **treatise** both the **Elements** themselves, as well as their action and effect, extensively enough (such that no **philosopher** has presented it more clearly to this day), so that a diligent researcher might more easily consider to what degree the **Stone** <term>the Stone: the Philosophers’ Stone, a legendary substance believed to facilitate the transmutation of metals and provide longevity</term> is distinguished from the **metals**, and the **metals** from the **Elements**. 

We have not written this **treatise** as if we wished to rebuke the **Ancient Sages**, but rather that we might further confirm their writings and fulfill what they omitted: since even **philosophers** are only human and cannot do everything perfectly <note>The original German idiom "nach der Schnur" literally means "by the string" or "straight as a line," implying perfection or exactitude.</note>, nor is every man sufficient in all things. 

Some have also derived miracles from the straight paths of nature, as is said to have happened to **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a Dominican friar and one of the most influential medieval scholars of the natural sciences; he was often legendary in alchemical lore.</note>, an excellent man and ingenious **philosopher**, who wrote that in his time small golden grains were found between the teeth of a skull in a grave. He was unable to fathom the actual causes of this wonder, but instead attributed it to a **mineral power** within the human being; and to support this opinion, the saying of **Morienus** <note>Morienus (or Morienus Romanus) was a semi-mythical 7th-century hermit and alchemist, said to have been the teacher of the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> occurred to him, where he says: **And the matter, O King, is taken from you** <note>original Latin: "Et res, o Rex, a te extrahitur." This is a famous alchemical axiom suggesting that the source of the Great Work is found within the human being or is of common origin.</note>

# **Prefatory Reminder to the**
# **Alchemical Reader.**

Since, according to the common proverb, good wares need no praise—or in the manner of the poet, "good wine needs no wreath," <note>original: "Raiffs... Ebhew kräntzes". Referring to the custom of hanging an ivy wreath or a hoop outside a tavern to signal that good wine was available.</note>—it seems entirely unnecessary to introduce many boasts and words of praise for this little book in this preface. For the book speaks sufficiently for itself and is renowned and well-known throughout most of Europe among scholars and lovers of this secret philosophy. <note>The "secret philosophy" refers to Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic" or "Hidden" art.</note> This can be recognized in part by the fact that it has been reprinted so often and so widely in various places and formats (which is admittedly a minor argument, considering that many worthless and even annoying pamphlets <note>original: "Chartecken", a derogatory term for cheap, poorly printed booklets or libels</note> are often published); but it is also recognized—and indeed to a greater degree—because it is highly praised, approved, and cited by the most excellent philosophers of this time: most of all **A ij but** <note>The "A ij" refers to the signature mark for the bookbinder; "but" is a catchword for the following page.</note>

# Preface

Now, no philosopher can do more than to set forth the Light and the Key. Whoever, then, has no eyes to see and no hands to unlock, or even lacks enough understanding to recognize and grasp the Light of Nature and the Key of the art from these treatises, he may surely be certain that he was not intended by God for such a high work, not dedicated by <note>original: *Fato*</note> Fate, and not qualified by his own nature. He should therefore—to avoid his own harm, the offense of his neighbor, and the slandering of the art—honorably withdraw from this endeavor, and instead more diligently pursue his other calling with the small talent <note>original: *pfündlin*, literally a "little pound," referring to the "talents" or coins in the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14–30).</note> granted to him by God.

# Preface

...the edifice of the world is maintained. One piece serves as the foundation, another as the pillar, another as the roof; one is useful, another merely ornamental. To this the highly enlightened Apostle Paul points when he writes of the human edifice or temple: <margin>1 Corinthians 12:17</margin> **If the whole body were an eye, where would the hearing be? If it were all hearing, where would the smell be?**

This is not said, however, to discourage or hold anyone back from their path and purpose, but for three reasons. First, to show that it is a gift of God, which cannot be attained by running <note>The original German "zuerlauffen" implies gaining something through frantic physical effort or "chasing" after it.</note> or by purchase, but is attained alone through praying, seeking, and knocking—just as all good gifts come from above, according to the double-triple <note>The author refers to the "double-triple" because the three-fold command (ask, seek, knock) appears in two separate Gospels: Matthew and Luke.</note> comforting promise of Christ: <margin>Matthew 7:7, Luke 11:9</margin> **to him who asks, it shall be given; to him who seeks, it shall be shown; and to him who knocks, it shall be opened.** 

That is the first reason. Secondly, it is to indicate the reason why so few in these present days reach the end of this <term>art<note>In 17th-century texts, "Art" (Kunst) usually refers to Alchemy or the "Great Work" of Hermetic philosophy.</note></term>, because they namely with un-

# **Preface**

# Preface

...to curse and damn those who improperly reveal anything of this secret to the unworthy. Likewise, no one should allow themselves to dream that this book contains everything within itself, and that he has no need to read others further: not in the least.

What reward such intentional swindlers and deceivers of the people are to expect one day is not difficult to conclude. # Preface

To those experienced in the **alchemical**, **mining**, **smelting**, **refining**, and **parting** arts: since this is the "ABC," which one must know perfectly backwards and forwards before one wishes to undertake this work. After the letters, one first begins to spell out syllables, and finally to read. For how will an inexperienced person understand the command that tells him to <term>dissolve</term><note>original: "solviren," from the Latin *solvere*. In alchemy, this refers to the fundamental process of "solution," or breaking a solid substance down into its liquid or essential form.</note> if he has not previously, often and well, dissolved all kinds of metals, minerals, stones, bones, and other natural growths? If he has not, it will go for him like a young boy who must first check in his primer to count out what kind of letter a character might be.

As for anything else that is not crafted according to every individual's sense, opinion, and thought, let him change it for the better, being assured that it all happened out of a good spirit, a faithful heart, and a well-meaning will, for his own affection, honor, and benefit. To that same reader, I herewith commend myself in the best form.

Written in <note>A city in the Black Forest of Germany, known at the time for its proximity to silver mines.</note> **Freudenstadt**, under the <note>Parnassus was the mythological home of the Muses; the author is claiming scholarly or divine inspiration for the text.</note> protection of Parnassus, on **Michaelmas Day** <note>September 29th, a major feast day in the liturgical and harvest calendar.</note>, in the year **627** <note>original: "627 according to the small, common, and shorter numbering." This is shorthand for the year 1627.</note>.

# Preface

...but also in these our times, the aforementioned special, lofty, Divine, and Philosophical blessing has not been hidden from or denied to certain people. I have not found it advisable to include my name here for specific reasons, especially since I do not seek my own fame, but rather desire to serve the lovers of Philosophy <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to "Natural Philosophy," the precursor to modern science, specifically Alchemical study</note> through this work.

Therefore, I gladly leave their vain desire for status to those who would rather be seen and regarded as something than to actually be it through their labor. What I have written here to testify to the unquestionable Philosophical Truth and have comprised in few words, I have drawn from the experience which the Most High has allowed me to encounter through manual labor <term>Handarbeit: This refers to "work of the hands" or practical laboratory experiments in an alchemist's workshop, as opposed to just reading books</term>.

# The First Treatise.

## Concerning Nature: what it is, and the character required of those who wish to fathom its secrets.

[Large decorative woodcut initial E] Many wise and learned people, many hundreds of years ago—indeed, even before the **Great Flood**, <margin>This art flourished before and after the Great Flood.</margin> as <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure considered the father of alchemy.</note> testifies—have written much concerning the preparation of the **Philosophers' Stone**. They have left us so many writings about it that—if **NATURE** did not perform such things every day (which are indeed believable)—hardly anyone would still believe that a **NATURE** exists at all. This is because, in ancient times, there were not as many people who invented new things. And indeed, our dear ancestors of old <margin>To observe Nature and its workings above all else.</margin> contemplated nothing other than **Nature** and what is possible within **Nature**. Even though they remained upon the simple and humble path and course of **Nature**, they nevertheless discovered such things that we in the present day—despite having such a vast abundance of many things—could hardly have imagined or devised in our own minds. This now happens because the nature and birth of all things in the world appear simple and contemptible to us; therefore, we, with our intellect...

<summary>This opening page introduces a series of alchemical treatises, emphasizing that the "Art" (alchemy) has existed since before the Great Flood and that true discovery comes from observing the simple, everyday workings of Nature.</summary>

# Concerning the high nobility and prominence of the excellent lineage of the **Wolfs von Wolfsthal**.

Although this widely renowned lineage of those of **Wolfsthal** has at all times been esteemed among the most magnificent and notable in the Empire of the German Nation <note>The Holy Roman Empire</note> since high antiquity, and therein has been held in great honors <note>original: "honoribus"</note>, dignity, and respect by the Roman Imperial Majesty and the Prince-Electors, Counts, Barons, and Knights of the Holy Empire, as well as other persons of high standing; and has also many times been employed by high potentates, such as the Imperial Majesty and Prince-Electors, in high offices, services, and commissions; and for those reasons became widely famed and recognized in the renowned **Society of the Unicorn** in the year 1445; likewise among the nobility in Bavaria in the year 1451; also in the **Tournament Society** in the year 1484; and similarly among the praiseworthy **Knighthood of the Rhine** in the year 1544; and otherwise in other places for their ancestry, name, standing, virtue, bravery, and faithful service.

Yet, over so many hundreds of years, as with many other ancient, high, and well-descended lineages, it has often happened—through the instability of this transitory life, as well as other unfortunate occurrences, the frailty of the world, and other causes—that those who passed away in old age did not diligently record their ancestry, genealogy <note>original: "Genealogiam"</note>, and family tree, nor previously commit them to writing or print. Consequently, they were easily forgotten by the youths who came after them, and thus fell into oblivion. This almost happened and occurred with this ancient lineage of those of Wolfsthal, had it not been sought out, inquired after, and researched through diligent inquiry, as well as the inspection of old monuments, grave inscriptions, epitaphs, and other credible old writings, and thus their origin and descent were in part brought back to light and recovered.

As will now be briefly recounted and described here in this following description, regarding how many men of this same ancient lineage could be found and researched according to their order and passing.

<summary>This page introduces the Wolf von Wolfsthal family, detailing their long history of service to the Holy Roman Empire and membership in elite knightly societies. It explains that the current record was compiled from old monuments and epitaphs to prevent the family’s history from being lost to time.</summary>

->### Of the Stone of the Wise 3<-

...reached the highest level. For the chemical art has discovered such subtle things that one could almost invent nothing higher, and there is such a difference between it and the ancient philosophers, just like between a watchmaker and a common blacksmith: for although they both work with iron, neither understands the work of the other, even though each is a master in his own craft. If in this day the father of all philosophers, Hermes <note>original: "Hermes" — referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the origins of alchemy.</note>, and the ingenious Geber <note>original: "Geber" — Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a Persian polymath and father of early chemistry.</note>, together with the profound Raymond Lully <note>original: "Raymund Lullio" — Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher whose name was frequently attached to alchemical texts.</note> should rise again, they would not be considered philosophers, but rather students by the <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymisten" — here referring to practitioners of the "Spagyric" or chemical arts of the early modern period.</note>. For they would know nothing of so much distillation, circulation, <term>calcination</term> <note>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to cause thermal decomposition or to remove volatile impurities.</note>, and the countless other works of the artists, which people in current times have found and devised from their books. Only one thing is lacking for us—that which we ought to know—what they were able to do and make: namely the **Philosophical Stone** or the natural tincture. While we now pursue this, we discover other things; and it would be no wonder, if the reproduction of mankind were not so common, and Nature did not maintain her laws so rigidly within it, that mistakes would also be made there <note>The author suggests that if human reproduction were as complex as alchemy, human intervention would likely have corrupted it by now, but Nature's laws are too strong.</note>.

But to return to my purpose: I have promised that in this first treatise I wish to explain what Nature is, so that a vain delusion does not [lead] us from the simple...

# The 1st Treatise on the <term>Stone of the Wise<note>Commonly known as the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and grant eternal life.</note></term>.

Take note in what way and by what means it is improved; so it will always be able to happen or be made in its like. For example: if you desire to bring a metal (as is our intention) to a higher <term>virtue<note>In this context, 'virtue' refers to the inherent power, quality, or purity of a substance.</note></term> than Nature has done, you must take a metallic nature and place it into male and female; otherwise, you will achieve nothing. For if you wanted to make the virtue of metal from herbs, you would work in vain, just as you cannot make wood from a dog or another animal.

## The Second Treatise.

### On the operation and effect of Nature in our intended <term>seed<note>original: "Spermate, oder saat"</note></term>.

Now I have taught that Nature is one, true, everywhere visible or apparent, <term>continuous<note>original: "continua"</note></term> or joined together, which is recognized by the things she brings forth, such as wood, herbs, etc. Also, that a researcher of Nature should be truthful, simple, patient, and constant, directing his thoughts and mind toward one thing alone, etc. Now we must speak of the working of Nature. Just as Nature exists in God’s will, and God has created her or incorporated her into every <term>imagination<note>Here meaning the 'formative power' or 'blueprint' of a thing.</note></term>, so also has Nature made a seed for herself—that is, her will within the elements.

<summary>This text explains the alchemical principle that like produces like, arguing that metals can only be refined using metallic 'seeds,' and defines Nature as a unified, God-given force acting through the elements.</summary>

This [Nature] is indeed but a single thing, and yet brings forth entirely different matters; however, she effects nothing without her <term>Sperma</term> <note>In alchemy, 'Sperma' refers to the 'seed' or generative essence of a substance, not just biological semen</note>. Whatever the seed wills, Nature effects, for she is like a master craftsman’s instrument or tool. Accordingly, the seed of any given thing is more useful and beneficial to the artist than Nature herself. For out of Nature, without the seed, you can achieve as much as a goldsmith can with gold or silver without fire, or a farmer without grain. Once one possesses the seed, Nature will soon find her way, whether for evil or for good. She works within the seed as God works within the free will of man; and this is a great miracle: that Nature obeys the seed, not by force, but of her own accord, just as God permits everything that man desires—not by compulsion, but by His own will. Therefore He has given man a free will, whether for good or for evil.

->**The Second Treatise**<-

<margin>The metallic seed or sperm, and how it is generated.</margin>
<margin>What the center of the Earth may be.</margin>

...imagination or the mental conception of Nature.
Just as a man’s seed has its center—or its vessel for seed—in the kidneys, so also the four elements, through their perpetual <term>motion<note>original: *motum*</note></term> (each according to its specific quality and nature), cast a "seed" into the center of the Earth. There, it is <term>digested<note>In alchemy, digestion refers to a process of slow heating and maturation to transform a substance.</note></term> and cast back out through that same motion. The center of the Earth, however, is a vacant place where nothing can remain at rest. Into the "ex-center" (as I call it), or the boundary and outer circumference of the center, the four elements pour their qualities. This is similar to how a man casts his seed into the <term>womb<note>original: *Mutter*, which literally means "mother" but was used archaicly to refer to the uterus or "matrix" of generation.</note></term> of a woman, in which no part of the seed remains [stationary]; rather, after the womb has taken its allotted portion, it casts out the rest.

In the same way, it happens within the center of the Earth that the magnetic power of a specific place draws to itself a substance that is suitable for it to give birth to; the rest is cast out to become stones and other <term>superfluity<note>original: *vberflüssigkeit*, referring to waste products or byproducts of the alchemical process.</note></term>. For all things have their origin from this same source, and nothing in this world is brought forth except from this spring.

For example: imagine there is a vessel of water on a flat table. This is placed in the middle of the table, and all sorts of things, various colors, as well as salt, etc., are laid all around it, each one separately. When one then pours this water out into the center, one sees that same water spread out in all directions. Whichever part of it touches the red color, that same...

->**On the Operation of Nature. 9**<-

...that same [water] becomes red; that which flows into the salt becomes salted by it, and so on. For the water does not change the places which it reaches, but rather the places change the water. <margin>The place where it is found <note>original Latin: "Locus ubi reperitur"</note></margin>
In the same way, the <term>sperma</term> <note>From the Greek word for "seed"; in alchemy, this refers to a universal life-force or "astral seed" rather than just biological fluid.</note> or seed comes out of the Center of the Earth, which the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> have poured into the Center. From there it goes into various places, and a thing is fashioned according to the nature of its place. If it reaches a place of pure water and earth, then the thing produced becomes pure as well. There is only one kind of seed or <term>sperma</term> for all things, yet it brings forth different results, as is shown by the following example:
A man’s seed is a noble seed, created solely for the procreation of a human being. <margin>All things have but one seed, but according to the difference of the places, it also brings forth different things.</margin> However, if a man were to misuse it—which he can do of his own free will—a monstrosity would result. For if, against the heavy and serious prohibition of God, a man were to mix himself with a cow or another animal, an animal could easily conceive from such seed, for Nature is a single unity. In that case, no human would result, but rather an animal and a monstrosity, because the seed would not have found a place that was suitable and appropriate for it. And through such inhuman and cursed mixing of humans with animals, animals...

10 ## The Second Treatise

...now that it has arrived in its place and has been received, it no longer changes its form. However, as long as the seed <note>original: *Sperma*; in alchemical philosophy, this refers to the primal "life-seed" or generative essence that precedes physical matter.</note> is still in the center, from that same seed can just as easily come a tree as a metal, a herb as a stone—and one more precious than the other—depending on whether the place from which it is born is pure or impure. 

Now we must speak of how the elements bring forth their seed, and this occurs <margin>How the elements generate their seed.</margin> in the following way. There are four elements: two heavy and two light, two dry and two moist—though one is very dry and one is very moist—and they are of male and female <margin>Genesis 28. 29. <note>This likely refers to the biblical command to "be fruitful and multiply," which the author views as a natural law governing elements.</note></margin> gender, etc. Each of these is of its own nature very inclined to bring forth such things that are like itself within its own sphere or circle, for so the Almighty has ordained it. 

These four do not rest, but one always drives the other, and each lets its own subtle essence or "subtlety" <term>subtlety: the refined, spiritualized part of a substance, as opposed to its gross, heavy physical body</term> go forth, and they come together in the center. But in the center is the *Archaeus* <note>a Paracelsian term for the "inner master" or vital force that directs the formation of life and minerals</note>, which in English might be called the "Originator" or the servant of Nature <margin>Archaeus, the servant of Nature.</margin>, who casts out these mixed seeds. However, how that occurs will be seen more extensively in the conclusion of these twelve small treatises.

12	**The Third Treatise**

<margin>Not the whole little grain, but the innermost spark of the same, becomes the Seed.</margin>

For the whole little grain or Body <term>Corpus</term> is not transformed into Seed; rather, there is within the Body only a necessary little Spark, which is protected by its Body from all excessive Heat, Cold, etc. If you have ears and reason, pay close attention here, and you will be safe, not only from those who do not know the place and location of the <term>Spermatis</term> <note>original: "Spermatis"; refers here to the seminal essence or reproductive principle of the metal</note> and wish to turn the whole grain into seed, but also from all those who occupy themselves with the vain dissolution <term>solvierung</term> <note>the process of breaking down a solid into liquid form</note> of metals, and want to dissolve the metals 

<margin>Vain labor.</margin>

entirely so that they might afterwards make a new metal out of their mixture. But if these people would pay attention to how Nature proceeds and advances, they would see that it happens quite differently. For no metal is so pure that it does not also have its impurity—one more or less than another. But you, faithful reader, will diligently observe the first point of Nature, as mentioned above, and that will be enough for you; yet I give you this warning: do not seek such a point in the common metals, where it is not found. For these metals—especially the gold of the common man—are dead, but ours are living and have a Spirit <term>Spiritum</term> <note>an animating life-force or vapor</note>; these must be taken in every way. For you should know:

<margin>Life and death of metals: what it is.</margin>

That the life of metals is fire, while they are still in their minerals and ore-mines; their death, likewise, is also fire—namely, the fire of smelting.

<summary>This page argues that the 'seed' of a metal is a tiny, protected spark within its body rather than the whole mass, and warns against trying to create new metals through simple chemical dissolution. It distinguishes between 'dead' commercial metals and 'living' alchemical metals, noting that while fire sustains metals in the earth, the fire of the furnace kills them.</summary>

This oiliness it later carries upward through <term>sublimation</term><note>The process where a substance is heated into vapor and then condenses back into a solid; here it refers to the natural purification process within the Earth.</note>, becoming an "unctuosity" or fatness. At this stage, it leaves behind the name of a "vapor" or "steam" and takes on the name of a "fatness" or "oiliness." When this substance later reaches higher elevations through sublimation—places already purified by the preceding vapors—where the Earth is subtle, pure, and moist, it fills the pores of the Earth, unites with it, and thus becomes **Gold**.

However, if this same "fatness" reaches an impure and cold place, **Saturn**<note>The alchemical name for the metal Lead, associated with the slow-moving planet Saturn.</note> is created from it. If the Earth there is pure but mixed with **Sulfur**, it gives birth to **Copper**, and so on. For the more a location has been purified, the more beautiful the metals become.

One must note that this same vapor is constantly steaming out from the <term>Center</term><note>The "Center" refers to the core of the Earth, believed by alchemists to contain a central fire that drives the growth of minerals.</note> toward the surface and the upper regions, and in its passing or moving away, it cleanses the area.

all the secrets to be found herein, it would fill a great book. But I return to my purpose and tell you what is true, my son: out of one, nothing can be born, for this power belongs to God alone; let it be enough that you can create one out of two, if it is of use to you. 
<margin>
The <term>Second Matter<note>original: *Materia secunda*; in alchemy, this refers to the substance once it has been prepared or "awakened" by the chemist, as opposed to its raw, "first" state in nature.</note></term>, what it is.
</margin>
Know therefore that the multiplicative and increasing seed is the second matter, and not the first; *for the first matter of things is not seen; it is hidden in Nature, or in the elements; the second, however, occasionally appears to the <term>Children of Art<note>A traditional term for practitioners of alchemy who have been initiated into its secrets.</note></term>.*

### Of the Power of the Second Matter. 27

<margin>Division of Nature into three kingdoms: Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal.</margin>

...even if the other two did not exist. There is the Mineral, the Vegetable, and the Animal kingdom. The Mineral can exist for itself, even if there were neither humans, nor trees, nor herbs. Likewise, the Vegetable [kingdom], even if there were no metal or animal, could exist for itself. These two are created from one, by one <note>This likely refers to God as the single creator, or perhaps a single primordial substance from which both arise</note>. The third, however, derives its life from the two aforementioned, without which it could not exist; it is more noble and glorious than the two previously named, just as it is also the last among them and rules over them, because power always ends in the third and increases in the second.

<margin>Rational man, the final work of creation. Genesis 1:26 <note>original: "Gen. c. 1. v. 26." This refers to the biblical verse where God creates man to have dominion over the earth.</note></margin>

Do you see this in the Vegetable Kingdom? The first matter <term>First matter (materia prima): In alchemical thought, this is the raw, original substance of a thing as created by nature, which humans cannot manufacture from scratch.</term> is a herb or tree which you cannot create; nature alone does it. In this kingdom, the second matter <term>Second matter (materia secunda): The substance derived from the first matter—like a seed or sperm—that allows for growth, reproduction, and multiplication.</term> is the seed which you see; in such a thing the herb or tree is multiplied. In the Animal Kingdom, the first matter is an animal or a human, the like of which you cannot create; but you know the second matter, or sperm, in which the human multiplies himself. In the Mineral Kingdom, you cannot create any metal; and if you boast of it, you are a liar. Nature has made this. And even if you already had the first matter according to the philosophers, it would still be impossible for you to multiply that central salt <note>A technical alchemical term for an essential, purifying salt believed to be the "center" or soul of a substance.</note> without gold. But only the children of the [Art/Wisdom] <note>original: "Kinder der" — The sentence cuts off, but "Children of Wisdom" or "Children of the Art" was a common way to refer to true alchemists.</note> know the seed of the metals.

<margin>3. What it is in the Mineral [kingdom].</margin>

28 **The Seventh Treatise.**

<margin>Seed and kidneys—in the vegetable, animal, and mineral kingdoms—and what they are.</margin>

The water in the mineral realm is the seed in the center of its heart and life; the kidney of its digestion is the fire. The receptacle <note>original: *receptaculum*; a vessel or container.</note> of the vegetable seed is the earth; the receptacle of the animal seed is the woman's womb <note>original: *Mutter*, literally "mother," but used here for the womb/uterus.</note>; the receptacle of the mineral water’s seed is the air. For this reason, it is necessary that the pores of the body be opened so that the...

<margin>In the center of the sperm lies the seed.</margin>

As long as the seed is in the body...

->34 The Tenth Treatise.<-

<margin>The difference between common gold and Philosophical gold.</margin> Gold which is digested to the highest degree: for common gold is like a herb without seed; when it becomes ripe, it brings forth its seed; so also gold, when it ripens, gives a seed or <term>Tincture</term> <note>In alchemy, a "Tincture" is a potent substance or "elixir" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or perfecting the human body.</note>. Someone might ask, why does gold or other metals produce no seed? To this, one gives the reason that it cannot become ripe because of the coarse air; it does not have enough heat. And it happens in some places that one finds pure gold, which Nature intended to complete, but was prevented by the coarse air.

<margin>Example.</margin> For example, we see that orange trees <note>The original German "Pomerantzen" refers to bitter or Seville oranges, which were exotic and difficult to grow in Northern Europe during this period.</note> do indeed grow in our lands just like other trees. In Italy and those places where they grow naturally, they bear fruit because they have sufficient heat, but in these cold places they do not bear at all; for when they should ripen, they remain stuck because they are overtaken by the cold, so that in these places one can never have fruit from them according to nature,

<margin>Gently and with skill <note>original Latin: "Suaviter & cum ingenio"</note></margin> although one sometimes comes to the aid of nature gently and skillfully, and in this way <term>Art</term> <note>The author uses "Art" (Kunst) to refer specifically to Alchemy, the "Great Art" of perfecting nature.</note> performs that which Nature could not have done. It is exactly the same with metals; gold can give fruit and seed in that it multiplies itself through the skill of a clever master who knows how to assist and further Nature. But if he were to undertake to perform it without Nature <insert>to accomplish</insert>
